[161:19.52] error-free. And if there's any error in
[161:22.40] your code, then basically the accept
[161:24.96] part gets executed. See, think of it
[161:27.68] this way. Let's say for example that you
[161:30.40] are writing some code perhaps for a
[161:32.64] project or an assignment. So you decide
[161:35.36] to put the main logic which is the core
[161:38.08] functionality or the essential part of
[161:40.32] your program inside the try block. Right
[161:43.04] after that you add an exception as well.
[161:45.84] So if there is any error, mistake or
[161:48.40] unexpected issue in the code you have
[161:50.32] written, it will display that particular
[161:52.72] exception to you as an error message.
[161:55.28] Right after that, it's an exception
[161:57.44] which is a special kind of event that
[161:59.68] will occur if something goes wrong. In
[162:02.08] that case, you need to put a print
[162:04.08] statement or any other action you want
[162:06.16] to perform inside the accept block. So
[162:09.36] if your code does not have any errors,
[162:11.84] the try block will run smoothly and
[162:14.00] display the relevant output whatever is
[162:16.48] required or expected. But if your code
[162:19.12] has some issues, problems or errors,
[162:21.84] then the control of your program will go
[162:23.92] into the accept block and whatever you
[162:26.40] have written there will execute.
[162:28.48] Whatever exception occurs will be shown
